## V9 coded acoustic tags

### Summary
V9 coded acoustic tags are acoustic transmitters operating at 69 kHz frequency that were used to track Northern Pike movements in this study. These tags were attached externally to 15 pike using cinch-up style tags (FT-4) inserted between pterygiophores below the posterior dorsal fin, allowing researchers to track fish movements for up to 7 days following catch-and-release ice angling.

### Context
> **Taxonomic & Life-History Summary**: [[Northern Pike]] (*Esox lucius*) is an important target species for ice angling in northern regions. In [[Buffalo Pound Lake]], pike can reach sizes over 110 cm total length and 15 kg. The study tracked 15 individuals ranging from 56 to 98.5 cm total length....
> -- *[[ChasingNorthernPikeUnderIceLon_2021]]*

## Vaejovis Chamelaensis

### Summary
A small scorpion species from the family Vaejovidae, described as new by Williams (1986) from the Estacion de Biologia Chamela in Jalisco, Mexico. Mature males reach at most 14.5 mm total length, and it is distinguished by its golden-yellow body, black carapace markings, five-subrow primary denticles, and a distinct subaculear tooth. It is known only from the type locality.

### Context

## Vagility

### Summary
Vagility is an organism's ability to move or disperse. The source lists it as a factor that influences endemism, with lower-vagility groups showing more endemism, and it also says vagility affects whether a taxon is endemic to an area at all. As an example, Anderson reports that volant mammals (bats) on Madagascar are only 32 percent endemic, far less than nonvolant terrestrial mammals at 94 percent.

### Context
> This review article examines how the size of a geographic area relates to the percentage of [[Endemism]] found within it. Anderson argues that the correlation between area and endemism must be positive, and that larger areas generally show higher degrees of endemism. The paper first addresses a...
> -- *[[AreaAndEndemism_PML0058]]*

## Val Verde County

### Summary

## Valdez and Ryel

## Vaquita
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The vaquita (*Phocoena sinus*) is a critically endangered porpoise endemic to the Upper Gulf of California, representing a key indicator species for the health of the Colorado River Delta-Gulf of California ecosystem. Though technically a marine mammal rather than a fish, the vaquita appears in fisheries and conservation literature alongside aquatic species like the totoaba because its survival is intimately linked to the same freshwater inflow, estuarine habitat quality, and binational water management decisions that affect fish populations in the region. Its decline reflects broader ecosystem degradation caused by damming and reduced freshwater flow into the Gulf, making it a symbolic focal point for conservation efforts that integrate fish, wildlife, and habitat restoration across the Colorado River system.

### Summary
A velocity barrier uses abrupt differences in water velocity to deflect fish away from an intake, relying on the fact that fish avoid sharp velocity changes. The source tested vanes set in a V-pattern, which deflected all experimental fish in one arrangement, and a 30-degree deflection with 10-degree vanes at 1.85 ft./sec., which recovered 92 percent of fish. ## Venard Lake

### Summary

## Verde Rim Fontelicella

### Summary
Verde Rim Fontelicella is an undescribed Fontelicella species endemic to Nelson Place Spring along the Verde Rim in Arizona. This hydrobioid snail was classified in 1981 as rare and of special concern due to its restriction to a single spring location.

## Verde River
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Verde River is a major tributary of the Salt River in Arizona that serves as critical habitat for native fish species in the Lower Colorado River Basin and Gila River Basin, supporting populations of federally listed and sensitive species including razorback suckers, roundtail chub, spikedace, and various native suckers. As a riverine system with relatively intact reaches and mixed fish assemblages, the Verde River represents an important conservation focus for desert aquatic ecology and native fish recovery efforts. The river's hydrological integrity and tributary connectivity make it significant for maintaining genetic diversity and ecological function in the arid Southwest's increasingly fragmented aquatic ecosystems.

## Verde River Aquatic Ecology

### Summary
The Verde River drainage supports several introduced bass species, with Smallmouth Bass abundant in Beaver Creek by 1956, Spotted Bass established in large numbers in West Creek tributary by 1949, and Rock Bass occurring in lower Oak Creek. Spotted Bass were introduced to the Verde River drainage by 1942.

### Context
> **Taxonomic & Life-History Summary**: This document covers multiple introduced bass and sunfish species in Arizona waters. [[Yellow Bass]] (*Roccus mississippiensis*) reached approximately one pound in weight in [[San Carlos Lake]] (1950) and [[Canyon Lake]] (1953), with larger individuals reported...
> -- *[[TheVertebratesOfArizona_1964]]*

## Verde River Basin

### Summary
The Verde River Basin is a drainage system in central Arizona, USA, and the setting of this review. The report assesses roundtail chub status across its upper and lower reaches, tributaries such as Fossil Creek, Oak Creek and Beaver Creek, and mainstem sections affected by diversions, grazing and nonnative fish.

### Context
> This Arizona Game and Fish Department (AGFD) technical report (Nongame Branch, Technical Report 114, April 1997) reviews the status, distribution and threats facing the [[Roundtail Chub]] (*Gila robusta*) across the [[Verde River Basin]]. ## Verduin Extinction Model

### Summary
A modified light-extinction model based on Verduin's 1954 and 1964 work, used in this study to derive an extinction coefficient of 0.07 m² g⁻¹ for suspensoids. It was combined with an extinction value of 0.03 m⁻¹ for pure water, taken from Whitney (1938). The authors used it to calculate suspensoid concentrations at depths where they had no direct measurements.

### Summary

## Vicariance Biogeography
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Vicariance biogeography in fish science refers to the process by which geographic barriers (such as changes in water availability, landscape fragmentation, or paleohydrological shifts) physically isolate populations of ancestral fish species, leading to allopatric speciation and the formation of sister species in different regions. This concept is fundamental to understanding fish biodiversity patterns, particularly in isolated aquatic systems like desert basins and inland seas, and has major conservation implications for reserve design and management of geographically separated fish populations that have diverged through geographic isolation rather than dispersal.

### Context
> - **Paleohydrology**: The passage describes a former landscape of permanent streams and clear lakes and great inland seas occupying basins that are now characterized by [[Interior Drainage]] and alkali flats — a hallmark of the Basin and Range desert topography of the western United States. - **E...
> -- *[[FishesOfTheDesert_1940]]*

> - **Allopatric (vicariant) speciation dominates**: Phylogenetic evidence suggests up to 71% of speciation events arise from geographic isolation of ancestral populations, producing sister species — a major driver of biodiversity patterns relevant to reserve design. - **Community cladogenesis**: W...

## Virgin Chub
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Virgin Chub is an endemic fish species found exclusively in the Virgin River, a tributary system within the Colorado River Basin, and represents a significant conservation concern due to its limited geographic range and specialized habitat requirements. Like other Virgin River endemics such as the Virgin Spinedace and Woundfin, the Virgin Chub exhibits specific adaptations to the river's historical water chemistry and flow conditions, including tolerance for elevated total dissolved solids (TDS). Its vulnerability to habitat modification, water management practices, and potential introgression from non-native chub species makes it a focal species for conservation planning efforts in the Virgin River ecosystem.

## Virgin River
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Virgin River refers to a specific river system in the southwestern United States (primarily Arizona, Nevada, and Utah) that has become a focal point for fish conservation and aquatic ecology due to its endemic and endangered fish species, particularly the Virgin River Chub (*Gila robusta seminuda*). This river drainage represents a critical conservation priority within the Desert Fishes Council and federal resource management agencies, involving habitat management plans, Wild and Scenic River designations, and Endangered Species Act protections to preserve native fish populations threatened by water development, non-native species introductions, and habitat degradation. The Virgin River exemplifies the complex challenges of balancing human water needs with the protection of endemic aquatic biodiversity in arid southwestern ecosystems.

### Context
> - **Bonneville/Virgin/Colorado system**: Upper Snake River tributaries were formerly linked through the pre-Bonneville Basin to the [[Virgin River]] and lower [[Colorado River]]. ## Virgin River Basin
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Virgin River Basin is a tri-state aquatic ecosystem spanning Arizona, Utah, and Nevada that serves as a critical conservation unit for endemic and native fish species, particularly the Virgin Spinedace. Within ichthyology and conservation biology, the basin is studied and managed as an integrated system where fish habitat reaches are classified as either "existing" (currently occupied) or "potential/restoration" areas, with management priorities focused on maintaining habitat integrity against threats such as water rights acquisition and other anthropogenic pressures. The basin represents a key focus area for aquatic resource conservation planning and monitoring efforts in the southwestern United States.

### Context
> - **Habitat**: [[Virgin Spinedace]] occupies stream reaches within the [[Virgin River Basin]] spanning Arizona, Utah, and Nevada; specific "existing" and "potential/restoration" habitat reaches are referenced as conservation priorities. - **Threats to habitat integrity**: - Water rights acquisiti...

## Virgin River chub
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Virgin River Chub is a small, native cyprinid fish endemic to the Virgin River in the southwestern United States that has become a focal species in conservation and recovery efforts due to significant population declines resulting from habitat alteration, dam construction, water diversion, and competition from introduced nonnative fishes. As one of the primary endangered native fishes of the Lower Colorado River system, the Virgin River Chub has been subject to various management and restoration initiatives, including habitat conservation planning and potential captive propagation programs. Its recovery represents a key conservation priority in Sonoran Desert aquatic ecosystems and is typically addressed alongside other endemic Virgin River species such as the Woundfin in comprehensive river management and endangered species recovery plans.

## Virgin River Corridor Area of Critical Environmental Concern

### Summary
The Virgin River Corridor Area of Critical Environmental Concern is a Bureau of Land Management designation that encompasses public lands downstream of the Beaver Dam Wash project area. This source indicates that the area also includes a Special Recreation Management Area and that the confluence reach is eligible for wild and scenic river designation under recreational classification.

### Context
> This biological opinion, issued July 22, 1996, evaluates the effects of proposed bank stabilization work in [[Beaver Dam Wash]], Arizona, on two endangered fish species: [[Woundfin]] (*Plagopterus argentissimus*) and [[Virgin River Chub]] (*Gila robusta seminuda*). The [[Natural Resources...
> -- *[[BiologicalOpinionOnTheEffectsO_11915]]*

### Mentioned In
- [[BiologicalOpinionOnTheEffectsO_11915]]

## Virgin River Database

### Summary
A centralized record of fish monitoring at multiple stations on the Virgin River since 1976, used by the authors to analyze native fish declines. The report re-analyzes this database, excluding 69 of 3,068 samples that were single-pass or had missing or erroneous data, leaving 2,983 for analysis. Earlier researchers used the same data but with differing metrics, and the reliability of the data was questioned at a 1995 workshop.

### Context
> This draft report by [[BIO/WEST]] re-analyzes the [[Virgin River Database]], a centralized record of fish monitoring at multiple stations since 1976, to explain declines in native fishes. The primary focus is [[Woundfin]], a federally endangered minnow, with secondary attention to [[Virgin River...
> -- *[[AnEvaluationOfChangesInNativeF_20471]]*

### Mentioned In
- [[AnEvaluationOfChangesInNativeF_20471]]

## Virgin River Depression

### Summary
A structural basin in the lower Virgin River area whose faults control the direction of groundwater flow and may convey deeper groundwater into the valley's alluvial system. The source identifies it as a key factor shaping regional flow paths.

### Context
> This 1993 addendum, prepared by the [[Las Vegas Valley Water District]] (District), updates an earlier hydrologic study of the lower [[Virgin River]] valley. It supports a proposed water diversion at [[Halfway Wash]] with a maximum rate of 700 cfs and a storage facility of about 113,000 acre-feet....
> -- *[[AddendumToHydrologyAndInteract_12989]]*

## Virgin River Exotic Fish Eradication Project

### Summary
A joint Fish and Wildlife Service and Utah Division of Wildlife effort, with help from the Washington County Water Conservancy District, to remove exotic fish from about 21 miles of the Virgin River. Rotenone was applied October 4 to 6, 1988, mainly to remove Red Shiner and protect the endangered Woundfin and the proposed endangered Virgin River Chub. This source reports a follow-up sampling memo on the project's effects downstream.

### Context
> This internal U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service memorandum (October 12, 1988, from Field Supervisor Sam F. Spiller to the Assistant Regional Director, Region 2, Albuquerque) reports limited follow-up work on the [[Virgin River Exotic Fish Eradication Project]]. The project used [[Rotenone]] from...

## Virgin River Fish Recovery Team

### Summary
The Virgin River Fish Recovery Team is a group concerned with recovering the river's native fish. It is among the recipients of this 1997 letter. The letter does not describe its specific work, but its membership indicates that native aquatic fauna are central to the basin recovery effort.

## Virgin River Fishes

### Summary
The group of three native fishes of the Virgin River system treated in the report: the Virgin River chub, woundfin and Virgin River spinedace. The source notes they make up 50% of the native fish fauna of that river, and that the Virgin River chub has declined for unknown reasons, woundfin survives only below La Verkin Spring, and the spinedace declined after introduced redside shiners increased and largemouth bass moved upstream.

### Context

## Virgin River Narrows
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Virgin River Narrows refers to a geomorphologically distinct reach of the Virgin River in southwestern Utah where the channel becomes confined and incised, characterized by narrower channel widths and increased depth relative to upstream sections. This section serves as a critical ecological boundary and sampling location in conservation studies of endemic Virgin River fish species, particularly the Virgin River chub, as it represents a transition zone between the upper braided, shallow reaches and the more confined downstream habitat. The Narrows functions as an important reference point for fisheries operations and population monitoring, with management activities often delineated by natural or constructed barriers relative to this geomorphic feature.

## Virgin River Recovery Team
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Virgin River Recovery Team is an interagency collaborative effort established to address the conservation and recovery of native fish species and aquatic ecosystems within the Virgin River system, which spans Utah, Nevada, and Arizona. This team coordinates monitoring, research, and management activities across multiple jurisdictions to support endangered species recovery, particularly for native fishes adapted to the Virgin River's unique hydrological and ecological conditions. The team's work integrates scientific data collection with adaptive management strategies to balance water resource demands with ecosystem restoration objectives in this sensitive desert aquatic system.

### Context

## Virgin River roundtail chub
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Virgin River Roundtail Chub (*Gila robusta seminuda*) is a federally endangered cyprinid endemic to the Virgin River system in the Lower Colorado River Basin, representing a desert fish species adapted to specific riverine conditions. This subspecies is of critical conservation importance due to its extremely limited distribution and apparent rarity, as monitoring surveys have consistently failed to capture individuals at survey sites, indicating severely depleted populations. Its management falls under the Desert Fishes Recovery Team's responsibilities, which coordinates conservation efforts for endemic Lower Colorado River Basin species facing threats from habitat modification, water management practices, and competition from non-native fish species.

## Virgin River spinedace
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Virgin River Spinedace (*Lepidomeda mollispinis mollispinus*) is a subspecies-level endemic cyprinid native to the Virgin River system in the southwestern United States that has become increasingly rare due to habitat degradation and competition from invasive fish species. As a focal species for conservation efforts in the Virgin River, this small native fish serves as an indicator of ecosystem health and represents a key component of the desert aquatic community requiring active management and protection measures. Its decline exemplifies broader conservation challenges facing endemic fishes in the Colorado River Basin, where habitat modification and non-native species invasions threaten native biodiversity.

## Virgin River System

### Summary
The Virgin River system is a drainage in southwestern Utah and northwestern Arizona with a highly specialized native fish fauna. Its threats include irrigation habitat loss, reservoir construction such as Lake Mead, and introduced species like the redside shiner and largemouth bass. Three of its fishes are covered in the report, making up about half of its native fish fauna.

### Context
> This report, reprinted from the *Proceedings of the Utah Academy of Sciences, Arts, and Letters* (vol. 51, part 2, 1974), catalogs the fish species and populations in Utah that are endangered, threatened, or of indeterminate status. It was prepared by the [[Utah Fishes Committee]] of the Bonneville...
> -- *[[ThreatenedFishesOfUtah_PML1074]]*

## Virgin River Woundfin

### Summary
The woundfin (*Plagopterus argentissimus*) is a small cyprinid fish of the American Southwest. The source describes the lower Virgin River population as the last reproducing population of its genus, and notes that a proposed U.S. Bureau of Reclamation dam would affect about 80 of the roughly 90 miles of suitable habitat. The authors argue that habitat changes are likely to be detrimental and should be avoided until the species' habitat requirements are defined.

### Context
> This document is a 1968 *Science* article by [[W. L. Minckley]] and [[James E. Deacon]] on the decline of native freshwater fishes in the American Southwest. The authors argue that declines are driven mainly by habitat change from water diversion, impoundment, groundwater pumping and [[Exotic...
> -- *[[SouthwesternFishesAndTheEnigma_PML1869]]*

## Virgin Roundtail Chub
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Virgin Roundtail Chub (*Gila robusta seminuda*) is a small cyprinid endemic to the Virgin River system in the southwestern United States, representing a distinct subspecies adapted to warm, turbid desert stream conditions. As a native fish species occurring in woodland and grassland stream habitats at intermediate elevations, it serves as an important indicator of riverine ecosystem health and is a focus of conservation efforts aimed at protecting native fish assemblages in the Colorado River basin from competition with introduced species and habitat degradation. This species is typically included in ichthyological surveys and endangered species management programs designed to maintain the integrity of remaining native fish communities in the arid Southwest.

## Virgin Spinedace
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Virgin Spinedace (*Lepidomeda mollispinax*) is a small native cyprinid fish endemic to the Virgin River Basin spanning Arizona, Utah, and Nevada that has been listed as an endangered species under the Endangered Species Act due to habitat loss, water diversion, and competition from introduced nonnative fish species. As a desert stream specialist, the Virgin Spinedace inhabits specific stream reaches within the lower Colorado River system where it faces threats from altered flow regimes downstream of Glen Canyon Dam, groundwater extraction, and degraded water quality. Conservation efforts for this species have focused on identifying and protecting existing populations and restoring potential habitat reaches through habitat management and in some cases eradication of competing nonnative fishes.

## Viviparidae

### Summary
Viviparidae is a family of freshwater gastropod mollusks (snails) characterized by their operculed shells and ovoviviparous reproduction, making them distinct from many other aquatic snail families in freshwater and brackish ecosystems. These snails are important components of benthic communities in aquatic habitats, serving as indicators of water quality and playing roles in nutrient cycling and food webs. In conservation and ecological contexts, Viviparidae distributions and populations are monitored to assess freshwater ecosystem health and biodiversity.

Fish that appeared in the hatchery ponds without being stocked, and which competed with stocked fish for food and preyed on young fish. At Drayton Plains the main volunteer was the brook stickleback (Eucalia inconstans), with about 113,000 found in one pond when it was drained, complicating assessment of fish production.

### Summary

## Vomer

### Summary
A bone of the roof of the mouth. In suckers, a cartilaginous rod joins it to the posterior condyle of the maxillary, forming a movable joint that the source identifies as part of the protrusion mechanism.

## Vomerine Dentition

### Summary
The arrangement of teeth on the vomer, a bone of the roof of the mouth, used by Hubbs as a key generic character. In Osmerus and related genera the teeth are canine-like and confined to the lateral tips, while in Spirinchus and Hypomesus they are numerous, convex and uniserial.

### Summary

## Von Bertalanffy Equation

### Summary

## Von Bertalanffy Growth Curve

### Summary
The Von Bertalanffy Growth Curve is an asymptotic mathematical function that describes how fish approach a maximum size as they age. This source presents a practical least squares method for fitting this curve to fish age-length data, providing objective parameter estimates (L∞, k, and t₀) with known statistical properties, replacing previous subjective graphical approaches.

## Von Bertalanffy Growth Equation

### Summary
The Von Bertalanffy Growth Equation is the specific mathematical formula l_t = L∞[1 - e^(-k(t-t₀))] that describes fish length at age, where L∞ is asymptotic maximum length, k is the growth coefficient, and t₀ is hypothetical age at zero length. This source uses Beverton's 1954 modification of this equation and develops methods to estimate all three parameters from age-length data.

## Von Neumann Ratio

### Summary
The von Neumann ratio is a univariate statistic for detecting serial correlation in a sequence of observations. The source names it as an earlier approach to testing autocorrelation, one that can be applied to autocorrelated error terms. The authors note that animal movements need bivariate or trivariate treatment, which led them to adopt Schoener's extension instead.

## VORTEX

### Summary
VORTEX (Version 5.1) is a Monte Carlo stochastic simulation model used for population viability analysis. It models demographic stochasticity, environmental variation, catastrophes, genetic drift, density dependence, harvest, supplementation, and metapopulations. ## VR100 portable receiver

### Summary
The VR100 portable receiver is a portable acoustic receiver with directional hydrophone used for manual tracking of acoustically tagged fish. In this study, it was used to detect signals from V9 coded tags on Northern Pike through 84-100 cm of ice, with detection range testing showing reliable signal detection at 750 m and some signals detected over 2.5 km distance.

## VR2 receivers

### Summary
VR2 receivers are acoustic telemetry listening stations deployed in an array of nine units throughout Stege Lagoon to detect tagged pike, with detection ranges of 100-500 meters. The receiver array provided full coverage of the lagoon outlet but only partial coverage of interior areas, with data downloaded monthly over the 18-month study period to track pike movements and residence patterns.

## VRAP System

### Summary
The VEMCO Radio-Acoustic Positioning system, an automated acoustic telemetry array that locates tagged fish from timed ultrasonic signals received by buoys. In this study three buoys about 400 m apart in a triangle recorded perch positions roughly every 180 s, with accuracy better than 1 m inside the array and about 10 m at 200 m from its centre.

### Summary

## Vrijenhoek Et Al. 1985
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Vrijenhoek et al. 1985 established foundational methodologies for using molecular genetic markers, particularly allozyme electrophoresis and DNA-based techniques, to assess genetic variation and population structure in fish species. This work became instrumental in conservation biology for identifying genetically distinct populations and management units, enabling scientists to evaluate the genetic health and evolutionary potential of threatened fish populations through molecular analysis. The approach has been widely applied in studies examining genetic polymorphism across fish populations and informing conservation strategies for species management.
